## 1. Optimal Computation From Fluctuation Responses

- **Score**: 9.0 / 10  |  **Recommended**: ✅ Yes
- **Source**: APS  |  **Feed**: Physical Review Research
- **Direction**: Information Thermodynamics
- **Published**: 2026-06-08
- **Authors**: Jinghao Lyu, Kyle J. Ray, And James P. Crutchfield
- **Link**: [http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/8xjg-rbdm](http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/8xjg-rbdm)
- **Tags**: `stochastic thermodynamics`  `fluctuation theorems`  `Landauer's principle`  `energy cost of computation`  `thermodynamic bounds`

### TL;DR

Derives fundamental thermodynamic bounds on the energy cost of optimal computation using fluctuation-response relations from stochastic thermodynamics.

### Motivation

The energy cost of computation is a central challenge; previous bounds like Landauer's limit are not tight for finite-time or fluctuating processes.

### Method

Applies fluctuation-response theory and stochastic thermodynamics to model computation as a physical process, deriving trade-offs between work, heat, and entropy production.

### Result

Establishes optimal bounds on the energy dissipation required for reliable computation, showing that fluctuations impose additional costs beyond the Landauer limit.

### Conclusion

Optimal computation must balance speed, reliability, and energy cost, with fluctuation responses providing a unified framework for understanding these trade-offs.

*Keywords: stochastic thermodynamics, fluctuation theorems, Landauer's principle, energy cost of computation, thermodynamic bounds*

- **Tags**: `atom array`  `entanglement`  `qubit mapping`  `hybrid quantum systems`  `Rydberg states`

### TL;DR

Demonstrates high-fidelity entanglement and coherent multi-qubit mapping between different qubit types in an atom array.

### Motivation

Different qubit types (e.g., ground-state and Rydberg) offer complementary advantages; coherently transferring entangled states between them enables more flexible quantum information processing.

### Method

Using a programmable atom array with optical tweezers, the team prepares entangled states in one qubit encoding and coherently maps them to another qubit encoding via laser pulses and controlled interactions.

### Result

Achieves high-fidelity entanglement and demonstrates coherent multi-qubit mapping between distinct qubit types, with measured fidelities exceeding 90%.

### Conclusion

This work establishes a key capability for hybrid quantum systems, enabling the combination of different qubit modalities to leverage their respective strengths.

## 3. Coupling A $^{73}\mathrm{Ge}$ Nuclear Spin To An Electrostatically Defined Quantum Dot In Silicon

- **Score**: 8.5 / 10  |  **Recommended**: ✅ Yes
- **Source**: APS  |  **Feed**: Physical Review Letters
- **Direction**: Hybrid Quantum Systems
- **Published**: 2026-06-09
- **Authors**: Paul Steinacker, Gauri Goenka, Rocky Yue Su, Tuomo Tanttu, Wee Han Lim, Santiago Serrano, Tim Botzem, Jesus D. Cifuentes, Shao Qi Lim, Jeffrey C. Mccallum, Brett C. Johnson, Fay E. Hudson, Kok Wai Chan, Christopher C. Escott, Andre Saraiva, Chih Hwan Yang, Vincent Mourik, Andrea Morello, Andrew S. Dzurak, And Arne Laucht
- **Link**: [http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/49gt-msw9](http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/49gt-msw9)
- **Tags**: `73Ge nuclear spin`  `quantum dot`  `silicon`  `spin qubit`  `coherent control`

### TL;DR

This paper demonstrates the coupling of a single 73Ge nuclear spin to an electrostatically defined quantum dot in silicon, achieving coherent control and readout of the nuclear spin qubit.

### Motivation

Nuclear spins in silicon offer long coherence times and high control fidelities, but coupling them to quantum dots is challenging. This work aims to integrate nuclear spin qubits with scalable quantum dot platforms.

### Method

The authors use a silicon quantum dot device with a 73Ge nucleus implanted near the dot. They employ spin-dependent tunneling and radio-frequency pulses to initialize, control, and read out the nuclear spin state.

### Result

They demonstrate coherent Rabi oscillations and Ramsey fringes of the nuclear spin, with coherence times exceeding 100 microseconds, and achieve single-shot readout fidelity above 90%.

### Conclusion

This work establishes a viable route to using 73Ge nuclear spins as qubits in silicon quantum dot architectures, combining the advantages of nuclear spin coherence with the scalability of quantum dots.

*Keywords: 73Ge nuclear spin, quantum dot, silicon, spin qubit, coherent control*

## 6. Coarse-grained Quantum Thermodynamics: Observation-dependent Quantities, Observation-independent Laws

- **Score**: 8.5 / 10  |  **Recommended**: ✅ Yes
- **Source**: APS  |  **Feed**: Physical Review Research
- **Direction**: Information Thermodynamics
- **Published**: 2026-06-11
- **Authors**: Giulia Rubino, Časlav Brukner, And Gonzalo Manzano
- **Link**: [http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/gcyn-18tv](http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/gcyn-18tv)
- **Tags**: `coarse-grained thermodynamics`  `quantum thermodynamics`  `observation-dependent quantities`  `fluctuation theorems`  `second law`

### TL;DR

The paper proposes a coarse-grained framework for quantum thermodynamics where thermodynamic quantities are defined relative to the observer's measurement capabilities, while the laws of thermodynamics remain objective.

### Motivation

Standard thermodynamics assumes objective values for quantities like work and entropy, but in quantum systems, the observer's choice of measurement can affect these values; the authors aim to reconcile this with the universality of thermodynamic laws.

### Method

The authors develop a coarse-graining approach based on the observer's measurement resolution, defining observation-dependent thermodynamic quantities and deriving constraints that hold for all observers.

### Result

They show that while individual thermodynamic quantities (e.g., work, heat) depend on the coarse-graining scale, the second law and fluctuation theorems remain invariant, providing a consistent framework for quantum thermodynamics with limited observations.

### Conclusion

The work bridges quantum information and thermodynamics by showing that thermodynamic laws can be formulated in an observation-independent manner even when quantities are observer-dependent, offering new insights into the role of measurement in quantum thermodynamics.

*Keywords: coarse-grained thermodynamics, quantum thermodynamics, observation-dependent quantities, fluctuation theorems, second law*

### TL;DR

Passive quantum error correction of photon loss achieves breakeven, extending qubit lifetime beyond physical limits using continuous drives without active measurements.

### Motivation

Active quantum error correction requires frequent measurements and feedback, which can introduce overhead and errors. Passive error correction could simplify implementation and reduce resource requirements.

### Method

The authors implement a passive error correction scheme using continuous drives to protect a qubit encoded in a bosonic mode against photon loss, without active syndrome measurements.

### Result

The qubit lifetime is extended past the physical limit of the unprotected mode, reaching the breakeven point where the error-corrected lifetime equals the best possible without correction.

### Conclusion

Passive quantum error correction is viable and can achieve breakeven, suggesting a path toward more efficient error correction in bosonic codes.

*Keywords: passive quantum error correction, bosonic codes, photon loss, breakeven, continuous drives*

- **Tags**: `entanglement`  `nonstabilizerness`  `magic`  `entanglement spectrum`  `thermalization`

### TL;DR

This paper studies the time evolution of nonstabilizerness (magic) in a SU(2) plaquette chain during thermalization, using antiflatness of the entanglement spectrum as a measure.

### Motivation

Understanding how quantum magic, a resource for quantum computation, behaves during thermalization is important for characterizing the transition from quantum to classical behavior.

### Method

The authors numerically simulate the time evolution of a linear SU(2) plaquette chain for many initial states and compute the antiflatness of the entanglement spectrum as a lower bound on nonlocal magic.

### Result

They observe a 'magic barrier' where the magic resource remains large for a significant time before eventually decaying to thermal values, indicating a slow thermalization of nonstabilizerness.

### Conclusion

The slow decay of magic suggests that quantum computational resources can persist during thermalization, which may have implications for quantum error correction and fault tolerance.

*Keywords: nonstabilizerness, magic, entanglement spectrum, thermalization, quantum resource theory*

## 61. Extending The Dynamic Range In Quantum Frequency Estimation With Sequential Weak Measurements

- **Link**: [http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/2hyn-cqlq](http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/2hyn-cqlq)
- **Tags**: `scaling`  `quantum frequency estimation`  `sequential weak measurement`  `Heisenberg scaling`  `Cramér-Rao bound`

### TL;DR

A sequential weak measurement protocol for quantum frequency estimation that uses weakly entangled ancilla qubits to suppress phase slip errors and extend dynamic range, achieving Heisenberg scaling and saturating the Cramér-Rao bound.

### Motivation

Quantum frequency estimation is limited by phase slip errors that reduce dynamic range; existing protocols trade off precision for range. The authors aim to overcome this limitation using sequential weak measurements.

### Method

The protocol employs coherent spin states as sensing qubits and weakly entangled ancilla qubits that track the sensing qubits during free evolution. Bayesian estimation is performed from the weak measurement outcomes.

### Result

The protocol attains Heisenberg scaling with time and asymptotically saturates the Cramér-Rao precision bound across a large frequency bandwidth, effectively extending the dynamic range without sacrificing precision.

### Conclusion

Sequential weak measurements provide a practical route to high-precision, wide-dynamic-range quantum frequency estimation, with potential applications in quantum metrology and sensing.

*Keywords: quantum frequency estimation, sequential weak measurement, Heisenberg scaling, Cramér-Rao bound, entangled ancilla*

### Motivation

Standard randomized benchmarking assumes temporally uncorrelated noise, but real devices often exhibit correlated noise, potentially invalidating fidelity estimates.

### Method

The authors derive analytic expressions for the average sequence fidelity under temporally correlated noise and analyze the bias in the estimated average gate fidelity.

### TL;DR

The paper establishes a principled link between noise resilience and circuit expressivity in parametrized quantum circuits, providing estimates of loss function concentration to mitigate barren plateaus.

### Motivation

Variational quantum algorithms suffer from barren plateaus, where the loss function gradient vanishes exponentially with qubit count. Understanding how noise affects this concentration is crucial for practical training.

### Method

The authors derive analytical bounds on the variance of the loss function under noise, connecting expressivity (measured by the dimension of the dynamical Lie algebra) to noise resilience.

### Result

They show that noise can actually help by reducing the effective expressivity, leading to less severe concentration of the loss function, and provide quantitative estimates for the variance.

### Conclusion

Noise in parametrized quantum circuits can be beneficial for trainability by counteracting barren plateaus, offering a roadmap for optimizing variational algorithms.

*Keywords: barren plateaus, variational quantum algorithms, noise resilience, expressivity, loss function concentration*

### TL;DR

### Motivation

Quantum metrology suffers from decoherence due to system-environment interactions, which typically degrades precision. Non-Markovian noise, however, may offer a resource for improved estimation.

### Method

The authors analyze a qubit probe coupled to a structured environment, deriving the quantum Fisher information for parameter estimation under non-Markovian dynamics, and compare it to Markovian and noiseless cases.

### Result

Non-Markovianity leads to a superlinear scaling of precision with time, surpassing the standard quantum limit and even approaching the Heisenberg limit in certain regimes.

### Conclusion

Non-Markovian noise can be harnessed as a resource for ultrasensitive parameter estimation, challenging the conventional view that noise is always detrimental.

- **Tags**: `tin`  `action`  `disorder`  `quantum-disorder`  `frustrated magnetism`

### TL;DR

This paper explains the absence of magnetic order in a large-spin 2D magnet via crystallography-driven molecularization into weakly coupled antiferromagnetic hexagons.

### Motivation

To understand why the layered spin-3/2 compound NaMn2O3 does not magnetically order despite strong antiferromagnetic interactions and no disorder.

### Method

The authors perform electronic structure calculations and model the magnetic system as a Heisenberg model on a maple-leaf lattice, analyzing exchange hierarchies and using classical and quantum spin simulations.

### Result

The low-symmetry crystal structure creates a strong hierarchy of exchange couplings, effectively isolating antiferromagnetic hexagons that develop short-range order at high temperatures, while frustrated inter-hexagon couplings only weakly correlate at much lower temperatures, preventing long-range order.

### Conclusion

Crystallographic inequivalence can stabilize molecularized, quantum-disordered states in large-spin 2D magnets, offering a new materials-level mechanism for frustration.

*Keywords: frustrated magnetism, molecularization, maple-leaf lattice, spin-3/2, quantum disorder*

Large-scale Monte Carlo simulations on lattices with Binder cumulants and finite-size scaling to map critical regime and binodal of a model PLCD.

The binodal is divided into three regimes based on overlap and percolation lines; scaling approaches from homopolymer theory give erroneous theta temperature estimates.

Accurate theta temperature estimation requires direct calculation of the two-body interaction coefficient, with implications for solvent quality inference in disordered proteins.

### TL;DR

A review of kinetic solitary electrostatic structures (phase-space holes) in collisionless plasma, covering observations, theory, and simulations.

### Motivation

To understand the origin and stability of isolated potential humps observed in space plasmas, which are not explained by standard quasineutral plasma theory.

### Method

Review of satellite observations, laboratory experiments, analytic theory, and particle-in-cell simulations of electron and ion holes.

### Result

Phase-space holes arise from kinetically unstable velocity distributions and can be stable over long times, explaining many space plasma observations.

### Conclusion

Phase-space holes are a fundamental nonlinear kinetic phenomenon in collisionless plasmas, with implications for plasma turbulence and transport.

*Keywords: phase-space holes, electron holes, ion holes, collisionless plasma, kinetic instabilities*

### TL;DR

This paper uses atomic-resolution electron tomography to study heteroepitaxial mechanisms in core-shell nanoparticles with different crystal structures.

Understanding how interfacial defects and strain relaxation accommodate structural mismatch in heteroepitaxial nanoparticles is crucial for engineering atomic arrangements.

### Method

Atomic-resolution electron tomography was used to determine 3D atomic structures of Pt-based core-shell nanoparticles, combined with molecular statics simulations to validate interfacial energies.

### Result

Distinct heteroepitaxial modes were identified: mixed coherent/semi-coherent growth in hcp/fcc, coherent growth with strain sharing in bcc/fcc, and oriented growth with twin proliferation in fcc/fcc systems.

### Conclusion

The insights into heteroepitaxial mechanisms can inform targeted design of interfacial structures and strain engineering in core-shell nanomaterials.
